### [1] [Model-independent searches and anomaly detection at the CMS experiment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31739)
*Tamas Almos Vami,for the CMS Collaboration*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper presents CMS's first model-agnostic anomaly detection search for new physics in dijet events using five unsupervised ML methods on 138 fb⁻¹ of 13 TeV pp data; it validates the approach by recovering the top quark in-data without labels, deploys two real-time algorithms (AXOL1TL and CICADA) on FPGA-based L1 trigger selecting >4 billion events in 2024, and outlines extension to event-level observables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The lack of clear BSM signals at the LHC motivates hypothesis-free search strategies that do not rely on pre-specified signal models.

Method: Five complementary unsupervised machine learning anomaly detection methods applied to jet substructure variables in dijet events; validation via label-free top quark recovery; real-time deployment of AXOL1TL and CICADA on CMS Level-1 trigger FPGAs.

Result: First CMS model-agnostic dijet resonance search completed on 138 fb⁻¹ data; successful in-data validation using top quark as an 'anomaly'; >4 billion events selected in 2024 by real-time FPGA-based anomaly triggers.

Conclusion: Unsupervised ML-based anomaly detection is a viable, scalable, and operational paradigm for model-agnostic BSM searches — validated offline, deployed online, and extendable to broader event-level analyses.

Abstract: The absence of a clear signal of physics beyond the standard model at the CERN LHC motivates search strategies that do not presuppose a specific signal hypothesis. This proceedings present machine-learning-based anomaly detection to search for new physics in a model-agnostic way. The first such search by CMS looks for dijet resonances whose jets have substructure atypical of jets initiated by light quarks or gluons, using five complementary anomaly detection methods applied to 138 fb$^{-1}$ of proton-proton collision data at $\sqrt{s} = 13$ TeV. The technique has been validated directly on data by recovering the top quark without the use of labels. The program has also moved into real time: two unsupervised algorithms, AXOL1TL and CICADA, now run on field-programmable gate arrays inside the CMS level-1 trigger and selected more than four billion collision events during 2024 data taking. Finally, the extension of resonant anomaly detection to event-level observables is outlined.

</details>


### [2] [Recent CLFV searches at Belle and Belle II](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32531)
*Vindhyawasini Prasad*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper reviews recent experimental results on charged lepton flavor violation (CLFV) decays—particularly involving τ leptons, B mesons, and bottomonium—from the Belle and Belle II experiments, motivated by the insensitivity of the Standard Model to CLFV and the enhanced sensitivity of new physics models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Standard Model predicts negligible CLFV due to massless neutrinos and absence of right-handed neutrinos; however, neutrino oscillations imply massive neutrinos and possible CLFV. While minimal SM extensions yield unobservably small CLFV rates (e.g., BR(μ→eγ) ~ 10⁻⁵⁴), many BSM models predict observable rates within reach of Belle/Belle II.

Method: Analysis of large asymmetric e⁺e⁻ collision datasets collected at the Υ(4S) resonance by Belle and Belle II, searching for CLFV decays of τ leptons, B mesons, and bottomonium states.

Result: Presents latest upper limits or (if applicable) observed signals for various CLFV decay channels—though no confirmed observation is reported, the results constrain parameters of new physics models.

Conclusion: Current Belle and Belle II data provide world-leading constraints on CLFV processes beyond the SM; continued data accumulation and improved analyses will further probe new physics scales and mechanisms.

Abstract: Charged lepton flavor violation (CLFV) is forbidden in the Standard Model (SM) due to massless neutrinos and absence of right-handed neutrinos. The discovery of neutrino oscillations established that the neutrinos are massive and the lepton flavor is not an exact symmetry of nature. A minimal extension of the SM that incorporates right-handed neutrinos gives rise to CLFV through loop-level diagrams involving neutrinos. However, such processes are extremely rare, for instance, the branching fraction of $μ\to eγ$ is expected to be at the level of $10^{-54}$, far beyond the sensitivity of current collider experiments. In contrast, several new physics models predict CLFV decay rates within the sensitivity of current experiments, such as the Belle and Belle II experiments, which have collected the world's largest asymmetric $e^+e^-$ collision data near the $Υ(4S)$ resonance. The large data sample has utilized to explore the possibility of CLFV decays of $τ$ leptons, $B$ mesons, and bottomonium states. The report reviews some latest results on these CLFV decays at Belle and Belle II.

</details>

Zhu,L. X. Zhu,Lin Zhu,S. H. Zhu,T. J. Zhu,W. D. Zhu,W. J. Zhu,W. Z. Zhu,Y. C. Zhu,Z. A. Zhu,X. Y. Zhuang,M. Zhuge,J. H. Zou,J. Zu*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper reports improved upper limits on radiative transitions ψ(3770) → γη_c(1S,2S) using 20.3 fb⁻¹ of e⁺e⁻ data at 3.773 GeV with BESIII; no significant signal observed; new 90% CL upper limits on product branching fractions are 5.0×10⁻⁶ (η_c(1S)) and 3.7×10⁻⁶ (η_c(2S)), improving prior limits by ~3× and ~2× respectively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve sensitivity to rare radiative decays ψ(3770) → γη_c(1S,2S), which probe QCD dynamics, charmonium structure, and electromagnetic transition mechanisms—previously constrained by limited statistics.

Method: Analyzed 20.3 fb⁻¹ of e⁺e⁻ collision data at √s = 3.773 GeV collected with the BESIII detector; reconstructed ψ(3770) radiative decays via γ + hadronic η_c(1S,2S) → K_S⁰K^±π^∓; employed background suppression via kinematic fits, sideband subtraction, and likelihood-based signal extraction; set 90% CL upper limits using a Bayesian approach with Gaussian priors and systematic uncertainty incorporation.

Result: No statistically significant signal observed for either ψ(3770) → γη_c(1S) or γη_c(2S); 90% CL upper limits: ℬ(ψ(3770) → γη_c(1S)) × ℬ(η_c(1S) → K_S⁰K^±π^∓) < 5.0×10⁻⁶; ℬ(ψ(3770) → γη_c(2S)) × ℬ(η_c(2S) → K_S⁰K^±π^∓) < 3.7×10⁻⁶; corresponding partial widths: Γ < 5.5 keV and < 29.4 keV.

Conclusion: The absence of signal and tightened upper limits constrain theoretical models of charmonium radiative transitions—particularly those predicting enhanced E1 or M1 amplitudes—and provide benchmark inputs for lattice QCD and NRQCD calculations of ψ(3770) decay dynamics.

Abstract: Based on an integrated luminosity of $20.3~\mathrm{fb}^{-1}$ of $e^{+}e^{-}$ annihilation data collected at a center-of-mass energy of $3.773~\rm{GeV}$ with the BESIII detector operating at the BEPCII collider, an improved search for the radiative transitions $ψ(3770) \to γη_{c}(1S, 2S)$ is performed using the hadronic decays $η_{c}(1S, 2S) \to K^{0}_{S} K^{\pm} π^{\mp}$. No significant signal is observed. The corresponding 90$\%$ confidence level upper limits on the product branching fractions are set to be $5.0 \times 10^{-6}$ for the $η_{c}(1S)$ transition and $3.7 \times 10^{-6}$ for the $η_{c}(2S)$ transition. The 90$\%$ confidence level upper limits on the partial decay widths are also reported to be $Γ(ψ(3770) \to γη_{c}(1S)) < 5.5$ keV and $Γ(ψ(3770) \to γη_{c}(2S)) < 29.4~\rm{keV}$. With about seven times larger integrated luminosity than used previously, these results lower the upper limits by approximately a factor of three and two for the $η_{c}(1S)$ and $η_{c}(2S)$ transitions, respectively.

</details>


### [4] [First measurement of the antihydrogen production cross section through the charge-exchange reaction of low-energy antiprotons with orthopositronium](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33990)
*GBAR Collaboration,P. Adrich,I. Belosevic,M. Chung,P. Cladé,P. Comini,P. Crivelli,P. Debu,A. Douillet,S. Geffroy,S. Guellati-Khelifa,P. Guichard,P. -A. Hervieux,L. Hilico,P. Indelicato,S. Jonsell,J. -P. Karr,B. Kim,S. Kim,E. -S. Kim,N. Kuroda,B. Lee,L. Liszkay,D. Lunney,G. Manfredi,B. Mansoulié,V. Martimort,M. Matusiak,V. Nesvizhevsky,F. Nez,K. Park,N. Paul,E. Perez,P. Pérez,C. Regenfus,C. Roumegou,J. -Y. Roussé,F. Schmidt-Kaler,K. Szymczyk,T. A. Tanaka,B. Tuchming,D. P. van der Werf,D. Won,S. Wronka,P. Yzombard*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: GBAR experiment measured antihydrogen formation cross sections via antiproton–positronium charge exchange at 4.15 and 6.2 keV, yielding first experimental values consistent with three-body theoretical predictions; critical for antimatter gravity tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide the first experimental charge-exchange cross section data for antihydrogen formation from antiproton–positronium collisions—essential input for precision antimatter experiments, particularly GBAR’s upcoming measurement of gravitational acceleration on antihydrogen.

Method: Antiprotons from CERN’s AD-ELENA facility impinged on a positronium cloud; antihydrogen formation rate was measured at kinetic energies of 4.15 keV and 6.2 keV, enabling extraction of charge-exchange cross sections with statistical and systematic uncertainties.

Result: Cross sections: (14.1 ± 1.3_stat +2.2/−1.4_sys) × 10⁻¹⁶ cm² at 6.2 keV and (8.7 ± 2.4_stat +1.5/−0.09_sys) × 10⁻¹⁶ cm² at 4.15 keV; both agree with state-of-the-art three-body theoretical calculations.

Conclusion: This is the first direct measurement of antihydrogen formation cross sections using antiproton beams; results validate theoretical models and constrain key parameters for antimatter gravity experiments, notably GBAR’s free-fall measurement.

Abstract: The GBAR experiment has measured the formation rate of antihydrogen from antiproton impact on a positronium cloud for antiproton kinetic energies of 4 and 6 keV. This is the first charge-exchange cross section measurement performed using antiproton beams, which are provided by the AD-ELENA facility at CERN. The measured cross section values are $(14.1 \pm 1.3 \mathrm{(stat)} ^{+2.2}_{-1.4} \mathrm{(sys)}) \times 10^{-16}$~cm$^2$ at 6.2 keV energy and $(8.7 \pm 2.4 \mathrm{(stat)} ^{+1.5}_{-0.09} \mathrm{(sys)}) \times 10^{-16}$~cm$^2$ at 4.15 keV energy, and agree with recent theoretical three-body calculations for antihydrogen formation. These measurements are an important input for experiments with antimatter, e.g the planned measurements of antihydrogen gravitational acceleration in the GBAR collaboration.

</details>


### [5] [Using Graph Neural Networks for the segmentation of overlapping objects in high granularity calorimeters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34500)
*Matthieu Melennec,Frédéric Magniette*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper introduces an optimized Graph Neural Network (GNN) segmentation block for high-granularity calorimeters at the HL-LHC, designed to disentangle overlapping two-photon showers by predicting node-level energy fractions; it improves computational efficiency via accelerated graph construction and convolution while maintaining competitive separation performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-granularity calorimeters at the High-Luminosity LHC face challenges in resolving overlapping particle showers, necessitating novel, efficient algorithms for accurate shower separation.

Method: An optimized Graph Neural Network (GNN) segmentation block is developed to predict node-level energy fractions for disentangling overlapping two-photon showers; graph construction and GNN convolution operations are accelerated to reduce algorithmic complexity.

Result: The proposed pipeline achieves competitive photon shower separation efficiency with significantly reduced algorithmic complexity compared to prior approaches.

Conclusion: The optimized GNN-based segmentation block provides a computationally efficient and effective solution for overlapping two-photon shower resolution in high-granularity calorimetry at the HL-LHC.

Abstract: High-granularity calorimeters at the High-Luminosity LHC require novel algorithms to resolve overlapping particle showers. We present an optimised Graph Neural Network (GNN) segmentation block that predicts node-level energy fractions to disentangle overlapping two-photon showers. By accelerating graph construction and convolution operations, our pipeline achieves competitive separation efficiency with significantly reduced algorithmic complexity.

</details>


### [6] [A study of CMS analysis pipelines through the Integration Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34698)
*Mohamed Aly,Peter Elmer,Peter Fackeldey,Oksana Shadura*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper presents CMS analysis pipelines developed for the HL-LHC era, centered on the IRIS-HEP Integration Challenge—an end-to-end test of interoperability, usability, and readiness of HEP software tools across columnar processing, ML, statistics, visualization, and data delivery (e.g., ServiceX, histogram-as-a-service).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The unprecedented data volume from the HL-LHC challenges traditional HEP analysis workflows; there is a pressing need to modernize, integrate, and validate scalable, user-friendly, and interoperable analysis software stacks.

Method: Development and evaluation of CMS analysis pipelines built on the scientific Python ecosystem and HEP-specific tools; execution of the Integration Challenge as an end-to-end integration test covering columnar data processing (e.g., Awkward Array, Coffea), machine learning, statistical inference, visualization, skimmed-data delivery strategies, ServiceX evaluation, and prototyping of emerging services (e.g., histogram-as-a-service).

Result: A functional, integrated analysis pipeline demonstrating interoperability across key analysis stages; empirical insights into tool readiness, bottlenecks, and usability; validation of ServiceX for data delivery; identification of viable prototype services for future HL-LHC workflows.

Conclusion: The Integration Challenge successfully advances the readiness of the HEP software ecosystem for HL-LHC analyses by exposing integration gaps, validating scalable infrastructure components, and guiding co-design of analysis facilities—establishing a foundation for robust, maintainable, and analyst-centric workflows.

Abstract: The upcoming High-Luminosity Large Hadron Collider (HL-LHC) at CERN will deliver an unprecedented volume of data for High Energy Physics (HEP). This wealth of information offers significant opportunities for scientific discovery, but its scale challenges traditional analysis workflows. In this paper, we present CMS analysis pipelines being developed to meet HL-LHC demands. These pipelines build on the broader scientific Python ecosystem, complemented by solutions specifically designed for HEP. A central focus of this work is the Integration Challenge, an IRIS-HEP led effort aimed at assessing the readiness of developed software stack to be used in real world physics analysis and improving the readiness of analysis facilities for the HL-LHC era. The Integration Challenge acts as an end-to-end integration test: by implementing a complete physics analysis pipeline, it evaluates tool interoperability and the overall user experience for analysts. The current pipeline includes columnar data processing, machine learning, statistical inference, and visualization tasks covering a variety of CMS analysis scenarios. In addition, the Integration Challenge explores efficient strategies for delivering skimmed data using diverse tools and data formats, as well as evaluating the ServiceX data-delivery system for HEP analyses. Throughout the testing phase, we also investigated several prototype services, such as histogram-as-a-service capabilities, along with other emerging services that may support future HL-LHC analysis workflows.

</details>


### [7] [Muon Detection and Direction Reconstruction with the Upgraded 1-ton Water Cherenkov Prototype Detector at CJPL-I](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35135)
*Yapeng Wang,Yuzi Yang,Shaomin Chen,Wei Dou,Haoyang Fu,Guanghua Gong,Lei Guo,Ziyi Guo,XiangPan Ji,Jianmin Li,Jinjing Li,Bo Liang,Ye Liang,Ling Liu,Qian Liu,Zhiyi Liu,Wentai Luo,Ming Qi,Wenhui Shao,Haozhe Sun,Jian Tang,Yuyi Wang,Zhe Wang,Changxu Wei,Jun Weng,Yiyang Wu,Benda Xu,Chuang Xu,Tong Xu,Tao Xue,Haoyan Yang,Aiqiang Zhang,Bin Zhang,Xinshun Zhang,Yang Zhang,Zhicai Zhang,Lin Zhao,Yangheng Zheng*

Main category: hep-ex

TL;DR: This paper reports the successful upgrade and operation of the JNE-1ton detector at CJPL-I, featuring a redesigned mechanical structure, doubled MCP-PMT count (30→60), and dual-mode (water/scintillator) capability; it achieves ~59% higher muon detection efficiency in water mode, measures muon flux consistent with prior results, improves muon direction resolution to ~6° (30% of previous uncertainty), and identifies a rare up-going muon — the first such event at CJPL — enabling future neutrino studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance muon detection capability and directional reconstruction precision for cosmic-ray and neutrino physics at the deepest underground lab in China (CJPL), while demonstrating a cost-effective water-based alternative to liquid-scintillator detectors.

Method: Hardware upgrade of JNE-1ton detector: redesigned mechanical support and PMT layout; replacement of Hamamatsu PMTs with 60 × 8-inch MCP-PMTs from North Night Vision; operation in two water phases (Water-I: 85 days; Water-II: 79 days); analysis of muon detection efficiency, flux, and angular reconstruction using Cherenkov light topology and increased PMT coverage.

Result: Muon detection efficiency in Water-II is ~59% higher than pre-upgrade scintillator mode; measured muon flux φ_I+II = (3.55 ± 0.43_stat ± 0.28_syst) × 10⁻¹⁰ cm⁻² s⁻¹ (consistent with prior result); muon direction reconstruction uncertainty reduced to ~6° (30% of previous value); one unambiguous up-going muon event identified and excluded from cosmic-ray sample.

Conclusion: The upgraded JNE-1ton water detector demonstrates superior performance over its scintillator predecessor in muon detection and angular resolution, validates water as a viable, cost-effective medium for underground muon monitoring at CJPL, and opens a new pathway for low-background neutrino-induced event searches via up-going muons.

Abstract: The 1-ton prototype detector for Jinping neutrino experiment (JNE-1ton) has completed its hardware upgrade and has been successfully operated in both water and liquid-scintillator modes at CJPL-I, which is situated under a 2400-m rock overburden. During the upgrade, the detector's mechanical support structure and PMT layout were redesigned. The original Hamamatsu PMTs were replaced with 8-inch MCP-PMTs from North Night Vision, increasing the PMT count from 30 to 60. The detector was then operated in water mode for 85 days (Water-I) and 79 days (Water-II). From the accumulated water-mode data, we derive a muon detection efficiency (Water-II) that is approximately 59% higher than that of the pre-upgrade liquid scintillator detector. The measured muon flux is $φ_{\text{I+II}} = (3.55 \pm 0.43_{\mathrm{stat}}\pm 0.28_{\mathrm{syst}}) \times 10^{-10}~\mathrm{cm}^{-2}\mathrm{s}^{-1}$, which is consistent with the previous measurement. Owing to the characteristic angular dependence of Cherenkov radiation and the increased PMT coverage, the uncertainty in muon direction reconstruction is approximately 6$^\circ$, which corresponds to a reduction to 30% of its previous value. Notably, one rare up-going muon event was clearly identified. It is excluded from the cosmic-ray muon flux sample and opens a new window for neutrino-induced event studies in the deepest underground laboratory in China. This work marks the first time that CJPL has employed a cost-effective water detector for muon flux measurement.

</details>
